People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep. I finally called the employee assistance program because I’m barely sleeping right anymore. I lie there replaying work stuff and arguments at home, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning. By the time I start the day, it feels like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive worrying. It’s more like stuff just gets stuck in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel tired or exhausted during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Most days I feel pretty worn out, like I’m starting the day already drained. Since I’m waking up around 3 or 4 and not really getting back to good sleep, it feels like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any panic attacks recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything where I suddenly felt like I couldn’t breathe or like I was losing control. At least, nothing I’d call a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or focusing at work or at home because of your lack of sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. At work especially, I used to get through sales forecasts or client decks pretty quickly. Now I’ll read the same slide over and over, second-guess the numbers, and still feel stuck on what I’m trying to say. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out at work or at home that you think might be contributing to your sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s mostly work stuff and some arguments at home. I’ll get into bed and just replay the day — things I need to do, stuff I messed up, conversations that didn’t go well. That’s kind of what finally made me call the employee assistance program, because I’m barely sleeping right. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and then the day starts with me feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when you wake up, such as always waking up at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s pretty regular. I tend to wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and then I’m just kind of awake, thinking about work or arguments at home. After that I don’t really feel like I rested, even if I was in bed for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring nightmares or disturbing dreams?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no. It’s more that I can’t stay asleep — I’ll lie there replaying work stuff or arguments at home, then wake up around 3 or 4 and just feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think about or worry about right before you wake up in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, usually it’s work, or something I said during an argument at home. I’ll kind of be half-awake already, replaying it in my head—what I messed up, what I still have to deal with—and then I’m fully awake around 3 or 4. After that I don’t really feel like I slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at the beginning of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights I do. I’ll get into bed and just lie there, replaying work stuff or arguments at home. That’s part of what finally made me call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping right, and even when I do fall asleep I’m up around 3 or 4 and start the day feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about right before you fall asleep that might be contributing to you waking up a few hours later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s mostly that I’m lying there going over work stuff, or arguments at home. I’ll keep replaying what happened or what I need to deal with, and then I end up waking up around 3 or 4 anyway. That’s really what pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m just not sleeping right, and I start the day feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work or at home that you think is causing you the most stress and contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s not one single thing, really. That’s what finally pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping and I start the day feeling like I didn’t rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0, G47.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep. I finally called the employee assistance program because I’m barely sleeping right anymore. I lie there replaying work stuff and arguments at home, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning. By the time I start the day, it feels like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive worrying. It’s more like stuff just gets stuck in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel tired or exhausted during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Most days I feel pretty worn out, like I’m starting the day already drained. Since I’m waking up around 3 or 4 and not really getting back to good sleep, it feels like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any panic attacks recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything where I suddenly felt like I couldn’t breathe or like I was losing control. At least, nothing I’d call a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or focusing at work or at home because of your lack of sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. At work especially, I used to get through sales forecasts or client decks pretty quickly. Now I’ll read the same slide over and over, second-guess the numbers, and still feel stuck on what I’m trying to say. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out at work or at home that you think might be contributing to your sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s mostly work stuff and some arguments at home. I’ll get into bed and just replay the day — things I need to do, stuff I messed up, conversations that didn’t go well. That’s kind of what finally made me call the employee assistance program, because I’m barely sleeping right. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and then the day starts with me feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when you wake up, such as always waking up at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s pretty regular. I tend to wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and then I’m just kind of awake, thinking about work or arguments at home. After that I don’t really feel like I rested, even if I was in bed for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring nightmares or disturbing dreams?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no. It’s more that I can’t stay asleep — I’ll lie there replaying work stuff or arguments at home, then wake up around 3 or 4 and just feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think about or worry about right before you wake up in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, usually it’s work, or something I said during an argument at home. I’ll kind of be half-awake already, replaying it in my head—what I messed up, what I still have to deal with—and then I’m fully awake around 3 or 4. After that I don’t really feel like I slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at the beginning of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights I do. I’ll get into bed and just lie there, replaying work stuff or arguments at home. That’s part of what finally made me call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping right, and even when I do fall asleep I’m up around 3 or 4 and start the day feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about right before you fall asleep that might be contributing to you waking up a few hours later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s mostly that I’m lying there going over work stuff, or arguments at home. I’ll keep replaying what happened or what I need to deal with, and then I end up waking up around 3 or 4 anyway. That’s really what pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m just not sleeping right, and I start the day feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work or at home that you think is causing you the most stress and contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s not one single thing, really. It’s the combination of work stress and arguments at home. I get into bed and start replaying all of it, and then even if I fall asleep, I’m up around 3 or 4. That’s what finally pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping and I start the day feeling like I didn’t rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. I’m definitely tense and my mind is busy, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it feels like anxiety or just… not being able to shut things off.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress you experience at work or the arguments at home always on your mind, or are there times when you can put those thoughts aside?\n\nPatient: There are times during the day I can push it aside, especially if I’m busy. But at night it comes back. Once I’m in bed, I start replaying work stuff or arguments at home, and then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and feel like I never really slept. That’s what finally made me call the employee assistance program.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulties in your social relationships or leisure activities due to your current state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I used to really look forward to things like pickup basketball, bowling nights, music festivals, or having friends over and firing up the smoker. Lately I either cancel, or if I do go, I’m kind of just there… going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that I can’t stay asleep — I’ll lie there replaying work stuff or arguments at home, then wake up around 3 or 4 and just feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think about or worry about right before you wake up in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, usually it’s work, or something I said during an argument at home. I’ll kind of be half-awake already, replaying it in my head—what I messed up, what I still have to deal with—and then I’m fully awake around 3 or 4. After that I don’t really feel like I slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at the beginning of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights I do. I’ll get into bed and just lie there, replaying work stuff or arguments at home. That’s part of what finally made me call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping right, and even when I do fall asleep I’m up around 3 or 4 and start the day feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about right before you fall asleep that might be contributing to you waking up a few hours later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s mostly that I’m lying there going over work stuff, or arguments at home. I’ll keep replaying what happened or what I need to deal with, and then I end up waking up around 3 or 4 anyway. That’s really what pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m just not sleeping right, and I start the day feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work or at home that you think is causing you the most stress and contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s not one single thing, really. It’s the combination of work stress and arguments at home. I get into bed and start replaying all of it, and then even if I fall asleep, I’m up around 3 or 4. That’s what finally pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping and I start the day feeling like I didn’t rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. I’m definitely tense and my mind is busy, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it feels like anxiety or just… not being able to shut things off.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress you experience at work or the arguments at home always on your mind, or are there times when you can put those thoughts aside?\n\nPatient: There are times during the day I can push it aside, especially if I’m busy. But at night it comes back. Once I’m in bed, I start replaying work stuff or arguments at home, and then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and feel like I never really slept. That’s what finally made me call the employee assistance program.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulties in your social relationships or leisure activities due to your current state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I used to really look forward to things like pickup basketball, bowling nights, music festivals, or having friends over and firing up the smoker. Lately I either cancel, or if I do go, I’m kind of just there… going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific happening in your life that you think started around the same time your sleep problems began?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It seemed to start when the arguments at home got a lot worse. That was… about a year and two months ago now, and the sleep problems kind of started around then too.\n\nDoctor: Did your sleep problems start around the same time as the increase in arguments at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s how it seemed to me. The sleep got bad around when the arguments at home picked up. I’d lie there replaying those arguments, and work stuff too, and then I’d wake up at like 3 or 4 feeling like I hadn’t really slept. That’s what pushed me to call the employee assistance program.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep. I finally called the employee assistance program because I’m barely sleeping right anymore. I lie there replaying work stuff and arguments at home, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning. By the time I start the day, it feels like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive worrying. It’s more like stuff just gets stuck in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel tired or exhausted during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Most days I feel pretty worn out, like I’m starting the day already drained. Since I’m waking up around 3 or 4 and not really getting back to good sleep, it feels like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any panic attacks recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything where I suddenly felt like I couldn’t breathe or like I was losing control. At least, nothing I’d call a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble concentrating or focusing at work or at home because of your lack of sleep?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. At work especially, I used to get through sales forecasts or client decks pretty quickly. Now I’ll read the same slide over and over, second-guess the numbers, and still feel stuck on what I’m trying to say. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that's been stressing you out at work or at home that you think might be contributing to your sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s mostly work stuff and some arguments at home. I’ll get into bed and just replay the day — things I need to do, stuff I messed up, conversations that didn’t go well. That’s kind of what finally made me call the employee assistance program, because I’m barely sleeping right. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and then the day starts with me feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when you wake up, such as always waking up at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s pretty regular. I tend to wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and then I’m just kind of awake, thinking about work or arguments at home. After that I don’t really feel like I rested, even if I was in bed for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring nightmares or disturbing dreams?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no. It’s more that I can’t stay asleep — I’ll lie there replaying work stuff or arguments at home, then wake up around 3 or 4 and just feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think about or worry about right before you wake up in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, usually it’s work, or something I said during an argument at home. I’ll kind of be half-awake already, replaying it in my head—what I messed up, what I still have to deal with—and then I’m fully awake around 3 or 4. After that I don’t really feel like I slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at the beginning of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights I do. I’ll get into bed and just lie there, replaying work stuff or arguments at home. That’s part of what finally made me call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping right, and even when I do fall asleep I’m up around 3 or 4 and start the day feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about right before you fall asleep that might be contributing to you waking up a few hours later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s mostly that I’m lying there going over work stuff, or arguments at home. I’ll keep replaying what happened or what I need to deal with, and then I end up waking up around 3 or 4 anyway. That’s really what pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m just not sleeping right, and I start the day feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work or at home that you think is causing you the most stress and contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s not one single thing, really. It’s the combination of work stress and arguments at home. I get into bed and start replaying all of it, and then even if I fall asleep, I’m up around 3 or 4. That’s what finally pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping and I start the day feeling like I didn’t rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. I’m definitely tense and my mind is busy, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it feels like anxiety or just… not being able to shut things off.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress you experience at work or the arguments at home always on your mind, or are there times when you can put those thoughts aside?\n\nPatient: There are times during the day I can push it aside, especially if I’m busy. But at night it comes back. Once I’m in bed, I start replaying work stuff or arguments at home, and then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and feel like I never really slept. That’s what finally made me call the employee assistance program.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulties in your social relationships or leisure activities due to your current state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I used to really look forward to things like pickup basketball, bowling nights, music festivals, or having friends over and firing up the smoker. Lately I either cancel, or if I do go, I’m kind of just there… going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific happening in your life that you think started around the same time your sleep problems began?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It seemed to start when the arguments at home got a lot worse. That was… about a year and two months ago now, and the sleep problems kind of started around then too.\n\nDoctor: Did your sleep problems start around the same time as the increase in arguments at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s how it seemed to me. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is my sleep. I finally called the employee assistance program because I’m barely sleeping right anymore. I lie there replaying work stuff and arguments at home, and then I wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning. By the time I start the day, it feels like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do think about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it excessive worrying. It’s more like stuff just gets stuck in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel tired or exhausted during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Most days I feel pretty worn out, like I’m starting the day already drained. Since I’m waking up around 3 or 4 and not really getting back to good sleep, it feels like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any panic attacks recently?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t had anything where I suddenly felt like I couldn’t breathe or like I was losing control. At work especially, I used to get through sales forecasts or client decks pretty quickly. Now I’ll read the same slide over and over, second-guess the numbers, and still feel stuck on what I’m trying to say. It’s mostly work stuff and some arguments at home. I’ll get into bed and just replay the day — things I need to do, stuff I messed up, conversations that didn’t go well. That’s kind of what finally made me call the employee assistance program, because I’m barely sleeping right. I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 and then the day starts with me feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any pattern or regularity to when you wake up, such as always waking up at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s pretty regular. I tend to wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and then I’m just kind of awake, thinking about work or arguments at home. After that I don’t really feel like I rested, even if I was in bed for a while.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring nightmares or disturbing dreams?\n\nPatient: Not really nightmares, no. It’s more that I can’t stay asleep — I’ll lie there replaying work stuff or arguments at home, then wake up around 3 or 4 and just feel like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there something in particular that you think about or worry about right before you wake up in the middle of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, usually it’s work, or something I said during an argument at home. After that I don’t really feel like I slept.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep at the beginning of the night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, some nights I do. I’ll get into bed and just lie there, replaying work stuff or arguments at home. That’s part of what finally made me call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping right, and even when I do fall asleep I’m up around 3 or 4 and start the day feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens or something you think about right before you fall asleep that might be contributing to you waking up a few hours later?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s mostly that I’m lying there going over work stuff, or arguments at home. I’ll keep replaying what happened or what I need to deal with, and then I end up waking up around 3 or 4 anyway. That’s really what pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m just not sleeping right, and I start the day feeling like I never really rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work or at home that you think is causing you the most stress and contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s not one single thing, really. It’s the combination of work stress and arguments at home. I get into bed and start replaying all of it, and then even if I fall asleep, I’m up around 3 or 4. That’s what finally pushed me to call the employee assistance program — I’m barely sleeping and I start the day feeling like I didn’t rest at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. I’m definitely tense and my mind is busy, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether it feels like anxiety or just… not being able to shut things off.\n\nDoctor: Is the stress you experience at work or the arguments at home always on your mind, or are there times when you can put those thoughts aside?\n\nPatient: There are times during the day I can push it aside, especially if I’m busy. That’s what finally made me call the employee assistance program.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any difficulties in your social relationships or leisure activities due to your current state?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I used to really look forward to things like pickup basketball, bowling nights, music festivals, or having friends over and firing up the smoker.
